RESOLUTION NO. __________ 
 
ENTITLED: "A RESOLUTION 
SUPPORTING 
THE 
CHEYENNE 
REGIONAL 
AIRPORT BOARD IN THE APPLICATION FOR AND ACCEPTANCE OF 
FEDERAL 
ASSISTANCE 
FROM 
THE 
FEDERAL 
AVIATION 
ADMINISTRATION UNDER THE BIPARTISAN INFRASTRUCTURE 
LAW TO ASSIST IN CONSTRUCTING A PASSENGER BOARDING 
BRIDGE CONNECTED TO THE PASSENGER TERMINAL." 
 
 
WHEREAS, the Cheyenne Regional Airport Board desires to participate in the Federal 
Aviation Administration grant program under the Bipartisan Infrastructure Law ("BIL") to assist 
in funding costs related constructing a Passenger Boarding Bridge ("PBB") that will be connected 
to the Passenger Terminal; 
 
 
WHEREAS, the Governing Body recognizes that the Cheyenne Regional Airport Board is 
unable to provide critical and essential services without obtaining available funds to complete this 
project; 
 
 
WHEREAS, the Cheyenne Regional Airport Board proposes to file an application with the 
Federal Aviation Administration for grant funding under the BIL for the PBB in the amount of up 
to Five Million Fifty Thousand Dollars ($5,050,000.00) over a 5-year period in entitlement funds 
under the new legislation to fund the Project; 
 
 
WHEREAS, the cost of the PBB is estimated to be Two Million Three Hundred Thousand 
Dollars ($2,300,000.00).  The final amount of the cost will be determined from sealed bids received 
in June and the design fees that have already been incurred by the airport; 
 
WHEREAS, Once the final amount has been established, then the airport will submit the 
final application for the funds. We anticipate that the FAA will issue the grant sometime during 
the summer months, but it could possibly be in September prior to the end of the federal fiscal year 
(September 30, 2023); and, 
 
 
WHEREAS, the grant is 93.75% FAA funded with the remaining funding provided by the 
WYDOT Aeronautics Commission at 3.75%, and the Cheyenne Regional Airport Board at 2.5%. 
 
 
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED BY THE GOVERNING BODY OF THE CITY 
OF CHEYENNE, WYOMING, that the City of Cheyenne supports the grant application to be 
submitted by the Cheyenne Regional Airport Board to the Federal Aviation Administration under 
the BIL in the amount of up to Five Million Fifty Thousand Dollars ($5,050,000.00) over a 5-year 
period to assist in funding costs related to the PBB. 
 
 
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED that if the grant application described in this Resolution is 
approved, the Mayor and City Clerk are authorized to sign the related grant agreement or 
agreements if spread over more than one grant, and other associated documents which may 
reasonably be required by the Federal Aviation Administration to finalize the grant(s) transaction.

CHEYENNE REGIONAL AIRPORT BOARD  
│BOARD COMMUNICATION COVER MEMORANDUM│  
 
DATE: 
May 2, 2023 
 
 
TO: 
City of Cheyenne, City Council 
 
 
FROM: Tim Bradshaw A.A.E., Director of Aviation 
 
 
RE: 
FAA Grant Application for Passenger Boarding Bridge (PBB) 
 
 
 
EXECTUIVE SUMMARY: 
The Cheyenne Regional Airport has submitted a pre-application to the Federal Aviation Administration 
(FAA) to construct a Passenger Boarding Bridge that will be connected to the Passenger Terminal.  The grant 
funds will be awarded to the airport through the Bipartisan Infrastructure Law (BIL) that was enacted by 
Congress in November 2021. The airport will receive approximately $5,050, 000 over a 5-year period in 
entitlement funds under the new legislation.  The cost for the PBB is estimated to be $2,300,000. The final 
amount will be determined from sealed bids received in June and the design fees that have already been 
incurred by the airport.  
 
Once the final amount has been established, then the airport will submit the final application for the funds. 
We anticipate that the FAA will issue the grant sometime during the summer months, but it could possibly 
be in September prior to the end of the federal fiscal year (September 30, 2023).   
 
The cost share for the project is 93.75% federal, 3.75% WYDOT, and 2.5% local (airport).  Once we have 
the grant awarded, the FAA requires “wet” signatures from the Mayor and City of Cheyenne General Counsel 
within 48 hours or we risk losing the grant funding.  A copy of the pre-application is attached for your review. 
RECOMMENDED ACTION: 
Authorize Mayor and City of Cheyenne General Counsel to sign FAA Grant Award when it is received by 
the Cheyenne Regional Airport

Paperwork Reduction Act Burden Statement 
A federal agency may not conduct or sponsor, and a person is not required to respond to, nor shall a 
person be subject to a penalty for failure to comply with a collection of information subject to the 
requirements of the Paperwork Reduction Act unless that collection of information displays a currently 
valid OMB Control Number. The OMB Control Number for this information collection is 2120-0569. Public 
reporting for this collection of information is estimated to be approximately 28 hours per response, 
including the time for reviewing instructions, searching existing data sources, gathering and maintaining 
the data needed, completing and reviewing the collection of information. All responses to this collection of 
information are required under 49 U.S.C. Section 47105 to retain a benefit and to meet the reporting 
requirements of 2 CFR 200; no assurance of confidentiality is provided. Send comments regarding this 
burden estimate or any other aspect of this collection of information, including suggestions for reducing 
this burden to: Information Collection Clearance Officer, Federal Aviation Administration, 10101 Hillwood 
Parkway, Fort Worth, TX 76177-1524. 
INSTRUCTIONS FOR FORM 5100-100 
PART I – Application for Federal Assistance 
Part I of the Application for Federal Assistance consists of a completed Standard Form (SF) 424. The 
remaining parts of Form 5100-100 (Parts II, III and IV) represent continuation pages that the Sponsor 
must attach to the associated SF-424 form. The signature of the Sponsor’s authorized representative on 
the SF-424 form represents acceptance of the representations and certifications made within the 
corresponding FAA 5100-100 form. 
PART II – Project Approval Information 
This information is necessary for the Federal Aviation Administration to evaluate this request for Federal 
assistance.  Responses do not require an explanation unless explicitly requested by the question. 
SECTION A. STATUTORY CONDITIONS 
Item 1 – Indicate whether the Sponsor maintains an active registration in the Federal System for Award 
Management (SAM).  Pursuant to 2 CFR §25.200(b), a Sponsor must maintain an active registration in 
the Central Contractor Registration repository (housed within SAM) with current information at the time of 
the application and during the active period of the Federal award. 
Item 2 – Indicate whether the Sponsor can commence the project within the same fiscal year the grant is 
made or within 6 months of when the grant is made, whichever is later. Attach explanation for negative 
responses. This information is considered when allocating discretionary funds. (49 U.S.C. § 47115(d)(2)) 

ii 
Item 3 – Indicate whether the Sponsor can complete the project without unreasonable delays. If 
applicable, provide listing of foreseeable events (winter shutdown, land acquisition issues, non-
aeronautical events, etc.) that have potential to delay completion of the project. (49 USC § 47106(a)) 
Item 4 – Indicate whether the environmental review (i.e. environmental assessment, mitigated FONSI, 
etc.) identified impacts or effects on the environment that require mitigating measures that lessen the 
impact or effect on the environment. If yes, provide a summary listing of mitigating measures. 
(49 U.S.C. § 47106(c)) 
Item 5 – Indicate whether the project covered by this request is also covered by an approved Passenger 
Facility Charge (PFC) application or other Federal assistance program by selecting all applicable check 
boxes (49 U.S.C. § 40117(d) and 2 CFR § 200.403). If the approved PFC application only addresses the 
Sponsor’s AIP matching share, select the appropriate check box.   
If the project, or portions thereof, is covered by another Federal assistance program, identify the Federal 
assistance program by name and the Catalog of Federal Domestic Assistance (CFDA) number.  
Item 6 – Indicate whether the Sponsor intends to seek reimbursement of Sponsor indirect costs as 
defined by 2 CFR §200.414 and 2 CFR Appendix VII to Part 200.  This information request does not 
include the indirect costs claimed by a for-profit entity (e.g. consultant). 

The de minimis rate may only be used if the Sponsor has not previously received a negotiated
Indirect Cost Rata (ICR) and does not exceed the limitations prescribed in Appendix VII to Part
200.

A Sponsor with an existing approved negotiated ICR must identify the ICR value, the name of the
cognizant agency that approved the ICR and the date of approval.
SECTION B. CERTIFICATION REGARDING LOBBYING 
This section addresses the Sponsor’s declaration regarding lobbying activities. The declaration made in 
the section are under signature of the authorized representative as identified in box 21 of form SF-424, to 
which this form is attached. 
Title 31 U.S.C. § 1352 establishes that no appropriated funds may be expended by a recipient of a 
Federal grant to pay any person for influencing or attempting to influence an officer or employee of any 
agency, Member of Congress, an officer or employee of Congress, or an employee of a Member of 
Congress in connection with this covered Federal assistance action.  Pursuant to 40 CFR part 20, this 
certification attests that the Sponsor has not made, and will not make, any payment prohibited payment 
by 31 U.S.C. § 1352.

1.
Compatible Land Use (49 U.S.C. § 47107(a)(10)) – Identify actions the Sponsor has taken to

3.

the airport is located to plan.
5.
Consideration of Local Interests (49 U.S.C. § 47106(b)) – Confirm the Sponsor has given fair

6.
Consultation with Users (49 U.S.C. § 47105(a)) - Confirm the Sponsor has consulted with

7.

b.
Has voting representation from the communities in which the project is located; or has

quality standards.
9.
Exclusive Rights (49 U.S.C. § 47107(a) – Identify all instances of exclusive rights to conduct
aeronautical services at the airport.
10. Land (49 U.S.C. § 47106(b)) –
a.

b.

c.
Complete this subpart when acquiring property interests under the grant.  Identify such
property by parcel number that corresponds to the associated Exhibit A property map.

PART III – Budget Information 
SECTION A. GENERAL 
1. Assistance Listing Number - Show the Assistance Listing Number from which the assistance is
requested.
2. Functional or Other Breakout:  Indicate “Airport Improvement Program”.  Prepare a separate set of
Part III forms for other Federal program categories.
SECTION B. CALCULATION OF FEDERAL GRANT 
When applying for a new grant, use the Total Amount Column only.  Use all columns when requesting 
revisions of previously awarded amounts. 
Line 1 - Enter amounts needed for administration expenses, which may include such items as: legal fees, 
mailing/shipping expenses, audit fees and documented Sponsor employee time that is necessary to 
administer the grant.  
Line 2 - Enter amounts pertaining to allowable preliminary expenses.  These include such expenses as 
independent fee estimate preparation, advertising expenses and permits. 
Line 3 - Enter amounts directly associated with the acquisition of land, existing structures, and related 
right-of-way.  
Line 4 - Enter fees for architectural engineering basic services. 
Line 5 - Enter amounts for architectural engineering special services (e.g. surveys, tests and borings). 
Line 6 - Enter fees for inspection, testing and monitoring of construction and related programs. 
Line 7 - Enter amounts associated with the development of land where the primary purpose of the grant 
is land improvement. Site work normally associated with major construction should be excluded from this 
category and shown on line 11. 
Line 8 - Enter the dollar amounts needed to provide relocation advisory assistance, and the net amounts 
for replacement (last resort) housing. Do not include relocation administration expenses on this Line; 
include them on Line 1. 
Line 9 - Enter the estimated amount of relocation payments to be made to displaced persons, business 
concerns, and non-profit organizations for moving expenses and replacement housing. 
Line 10 - Enter the cost of demolition or removal of improvements on developed land. Reduce the costs 
on this line by the amount of expected proceeds from the sale of salvage, if so instructed by the Federal 
grantor agency. Otherwise, show the proceeds on Line 15. 
Line 11 - Enter amounts for the actual construction of, addition to or restoration of a facility. Include in this 
category the amounts of project improvements such as grading, drainage, paving, marking, lighting, 
buildings, seeding/sodding, etc. 
Line 12 - Enter amounts for equipment. Examples include ARFF vehicles, SRE equipment, AWOS 
equipment, interactive training, NAVAID equipment, etc.) 
Line 13 - Enter miscellaneous amounts for items not specifically covered by previous categories. 

FAA Form 5100-100 
v 
Line 14 - Enter the sum of Lines 1-13. 
Line 15 - Enter the estimated amount of program income that will be earned during the grant period and 
applied to the program. Examples include vehicle trade-in value, sale of millings resulting from project, 
credits passed on from contractor, etc. This line may be used to indicate applied liquidated damages. 
Line 16 - Enter the difference between Line 14 and Line 15. 
Line 17 - Enter the aggregate amount for those items, which are a part of the project but not subject to 
Federal participation. Refer to Section C, exclusions. 
Line 18 – Enter the subtotal sum of Lines 16 and 17. (This is the amount to which the matching share 
ratio prescribed in program legislation is applied.) 
Line 19 - Indicate the total amount of the Federal assistance requested. This value is determined by 
multiplying the grant participation rate by the amount indicated in line 18. 
Line 20 – Indicate the amount of the Grantee’s share (from Section D). 
Line 21 – Indicate the amount of other shares (from Section D) 
Line 22 – Indicate sum of Lines 19, 20 and 21. 
SECTION C. EXCLUSIONS 
Line 23 a-g - Identify and list those costs which are part of the project cost but are not subject to Federal 
participation because of program legislation or Federal grantor agency instructions. The total amount on 
Line g should agree with the amount shown on Line 17 of Section B. 
SECTION D. PROPOSED METHOD OF FINANCING NON-FEDERAL SHARE 
Line 24 a-g - Show the source of the grantee’s share. If cash is not immediately available, specify the 
actions completed to date and those actions remaining to make cash available under Section E - 
Remarks. Indicate also the period of time that will be required after execution of the grant agreement to 
obtain the funds. If there is a non-cash contribution, explain what this contribution will consist of. 
Line 24h - Indicate total of Lines 24 a-g. This amount must equal the amount in Section B, Line 20. 
Line 25a - Show the amount that will be contributed by a State or state agency, only if the applicant is not 
a State or state agency. If there is a non-cash or other contribution, explain what the contribution will 
consist of under Section E - Remarks. 
Line 25b - Show the amount that will be contributed from other sources. If there is a non-cash 
contribution, explain what the contribution will consist of under Section E - Remarks. 
Line 25c - Show the total of Lines 28a and 28b. This amount must be the same as the amount shown in 
Section B, Line 21. 
Line 26 - Enter the totals of Lines 24h and 25c. 
SECTION E. OTHER REMARKS 
Make any remarks pertinent to the project and provide any other information required by these 
instructions or the grantor agency. Attach additional sheets, if necessary.  

FAA Form 5100-100 
vi 
PART IV – Program Narrative
Prepare the program narrative statement in accordance with the following instructions for all new grant 
programs. Requests for supplemental assistance should be responsive to Item 5b only. Requests for 
continuation or refunding or other changes of an approved project should be responsive to Item 5c only. 
1.
OBJECTIVES AND NEED FOR THIS ASSISTANCE
Provide a short and concise description of the proposed improvement. Include a narrative on why this 
improvement is needed. 
2.
RESULTS OR BENEFITS EXPECTED
Identify results and benefits to be derived. For example, include a description of who will occupy the 
facility and show how the facility will be used. For land acquisition or development projects, explain how 
the project will benefit the public. 
3.
APPROACH
a.
Outline a plan of action pertaining to the scope and detail of how the Sponsor proposes to
accomplish the work.
b.
Cite factors, which might accelerate or decelerate the work, and your reason for taking this
approach as opposed to others. Describe any unusual features of the project such as
construction approach, reductions in cost or time or extraordinary social and community
involvements.
c.
Provide projections of project milestone dates. As a minimum, identify target dates for defining
project costs (i.e. bid opening or completion of negotiations), anticipated issuance of notice-to-
proceed and anticipated project completion date.
d.
Identify monitoring and oversight mechanisms the Sponsor proposes to implement.
e.
List key individuals and entities such as consultant, Sponsor personnel and contractor who will
work on the project. Provide a short description of the nature of their effort or contribution.
4.
GEOGRAPHIC LOCATION
Identify location of the project. This will typically be the name of the airport.
5.
IF APPLICABLE, PROVIDE THE FOLLOWING INFORMATION:
a.
Describe the relationship between this project and other work planned, anticipated or underway
under the Federal Assistance listed under Part II, Section A, Item 5.
b.
Explain the reason for all requests for supplemental assistance and justify the need for additional
funding.
c.
If there have been significant changes in the project objectives, location, approach or time delays,
explain and justify. For other requests for changes or amendments, explain the reason for the
change(s). If the scope, budget, or objectives have changed or an extension of time is necessary,
explain the circumstances and justify.
6.
SPONSOR’S REPRESENTATIVE
Identify contact information of Sponsor’s representative.

3. Possible Disabilities – There are no facts or circumstances (including the existence of effective or proposed leases,

development of the area surrounding the airport.
5. Consideration of Local Interest – It has given fair consideration to the interest of communities in or near where the
project may be located.
6. Consultation with Users – In making a decision to undertake an airport development project under Title 49, United
States Code, it has consulted with airport users that will potentially be affected by the project (§ 47105(a)(2)).
7. Public Hearings – In projects involving the location of an airport, an airport runway or a major runway extension, it
has afforded the opportunity for public hearings for the purpose of considering the economic, social, and environmental
effects of the airport or runway location and its consistency with goals and objectives of such planning as has been

hearings to the Secretary. Further, for such projects, it has on its management board either voting representation from
the communities where the project is located or has advised the communities that they have the right to petition the
Secretary concerning a proposed project.

quality standards. In any case where such standards have not been approved and where applicable air and water
quality standards have been promulgated by the Administrator of the Environmental Protection Agency, certification
shall be obtained from such Administrator. Notice of certification or refusal to certify shall be provided within sixty days
after the project application has been received by the Secretary.

Airport Terminal Passenger Boarding Bridge
Cheyenne Regional Airport
The Airport intends to install a passenger boarding bridge (PBB) on new terminal building.
Protects passengers from the elements during enplaning and deplaning, thereby bettering ADA accessibility and the
safety of passengers and workers.  Ground boarding of aircraft via the apron and air stairs or similar equipment
entails passengers using surfaces that can be slippery due to rain or subject to movement due to high Wyoming
winds. Provision of a fixed tunnel and boarding bridge for this operation is a safety enhancement.  The use of a
boarding bridge means enhancement of the boarding experience for flying public.
The project will utilize traditional design-bid-build methods for construction.
Cheyenne, Laramie County, Wyoming

WHEREAS, the Downtown Development Authority ("DDA") was created pursuant to 
Ordinance No. 2804 adopted by the Governing Body on July 9, 1984; 
 
WHEREAS, Wyo. Stat.§ 15-9-208(a) requires the DDA to propose a budget, which shall 
be administratively reviewed by the City prior to submission of the proposed budget to the 
Governing Body for approval; 
 
WHEREAS, Cheyenne City Code, Chapter 2. 76.050(C), provides that the DDA budget 
shall be subject to alteration, modification and approval by the Governing Body in accordance 
with the policies of the City; and, 
 
WHEREAS, City staff has reviewed the proposed budget received from the DDA.  
 
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED BY THE GOVERNING BODY OF THE CITY 
OF CHEYENNE, WYOMING, that the proposed budget for the Fiscal Year beginning July 1, 
2023 and ending June 30, 2024 of the Downtown Development Authority, as set forth in the 
attached information with this Resolution entitled "Cheyenne Downtown Development Authority 
Fiscal Year 2024 Proposed Budget" in the total amount of $1,202,000.00, is hereby approved.

CITY OF CHEYENNE GOVERNING BODY 
MAY 22, 2023 
STAFF REPORT 
 
FILE: 
UDC-23-00339:  Ironhorse 3rd Filing Outside User Agreement  
REQUEST: 
An outside user agreement for the provision of water for a clustered 
residential subdivision of approximately thirty-seven (37) lots which includes 
thirty-three (33) residential, three (3) open space, and one (1) vacant lot 
LOCATION: 
Southeast of the intersection of Happy Jack Road and Bruns Way 
APPLICANT: 
Ironhorse Capital, Limited Partnership 
AGENT: 
Casey Palma, Palma Land Planning  
OWNERS: 
Ironhorse Capital, Limited Partnership 
PREPARED BY: 
Charles W. Bloom, AICP, Planning and Development Director 
 
 
  
  
  
  
  
  
  
  Planning and Development Department 
2101 O’Neil Avenue, Suite 202, Cheyenne WY 82001 
(Phone) 307-637-6282 
(Fax) 307-637-6366 

UDC-23-00339 
 
 
May 22, 2023 
 
 
APPLICABLE CITY CODE SECTION(S) AND PLANS: 
 
• 
PlanCheyenne 
• 
Resolution 3577 
 
BACKGROUND: 
 
The applicant is requesting connection to the BOPU water system for property outside City limits for a 
subdivision to be known as Ironhorse 3rd Filing, Laramie County, WY.  The proposed subdivision is comprised 
of approximately 97.93-acres and proposes a clustered residential subdivision of approximately thirty-seven 
(37) lots which includes thirty-three (33) residential lots, three (3) open space lots, and one (1) vacant lot.  
The subdivision is approximately 1,300 feet north of City limits.   
 
The County Planning Commission Staff Report is attached. The County Planning Commission considered this 
item on May 11, 2023, and recommended approval subject to conditions, including a condition that this 
outside user agreement was approved.  The next step is for the applicant to submit a final plat for review by 
County Staff, the County Planning Commission, and the Board of County Commissioners.   
 
For details regarding the development, please see the County Planning Commission Staff report which is 
attached.   
 
ANALYSIS OF APPLICATION CONFORMANCE WITH REVIEW CRITERIA: 
 
The request for an outside user agreement has no formal review criteria; however, the following 
elements should be considered when reviewing outside user agreements: 
- 
BOPU capacity 
- 
Needed infrastructure improvements 
- 
Population served 
- 
Considerations on the effect on future annexation(s) 
- 
Fiscal cost/benefit to the City and the BOPU 
- 
Conformance with the Comprehensive Plan  
 
BOPU has identified that capacity exists to provide service to the subdivision.   The applicant proposes 
constructing a private system that will be maintained by a private district.  Typical water usage for a 
single-family home is approximately 6,000 per month and commercial development heavily depends on the 
end user. Attached is the Board of Public Utilities’ (BOPU) flow calculation worksheet for this subdivision.   
 
County development regulations require lots to have a minimum lot area of 5.25 acres.  With this 
standard, the area within the plat boundary could support between 10 and 12 residential lots, 
depending on right-of-way dedication.  Provision of an outside user agreement allows for the developer 
to develop an additional 21-23 single family homes and a potential commercial area. At present, the City 
has sufficient system capacity to provide that additional capacity, however granting of this agreement 
would decrease the ability of the City to allocate that water to properties within the City limits, whether 
needed or not.    
 
The private system will be built to a rural system standard and will be maintained by the special district. 
The special district will be responsible for all maintenance and repairs.  The proposed service line will be 
installed across lot frontages that are not in the district.  In the event any other property owners seek to 

UDC-23-00339 
 
 
May 22, 2023 
connect to the private sytem, the district would have to be expanded and the system would have to be 
evaluated by the BOPU and Wyoming Department of Environmental Quality (WDEQ) standards.  If 
annexed, the sytem would require upgrading to City standards.   
 
In April of 2023, the Center for Business and Economic Analysis (CBEA) at the University of Wyoming 
evaluated the costs to the City for providing municipal water to County developments.  The study was 
entitled “Evaluating the Economic Impact of Providing Municipal Water to Laramie County Housing 
Developments” (Study) and concluded that “for nearly all the parameter values, it does not make sense 
to provide municipal water to Laramie County new housing developments” (Study, p. 5).  
 
4% sales tax revenue, 1% sales tax revenue, gas tax revenue, severance taxes, and State direct 
distribution are all dependent on a City’s percentage of the overall population of the County. The Study 
concluded that for each additional person added to the incorporated County, the City lost $626 per new 
resident.   With the average household size being 2.29 persons per household, this translates into 
$1,433.54 per household.  This agreement would enable at least 21-23 additional units which translates 
into $30,104.34 and $ 32,971.42, respectively, annually for just the residential component of the 
subdivision.  The Study’s parameters looked at the cost to develop a ten-lot subdivision on a five-acre lot 
which would yield lots averaging approximately 21,780 sq. ft., which is just over twice as dense as the 
residential lot sizes proposed within Ironhorse 3rd Filing.      
 
Property taxes would increase; however, the increased property tax would benefit the County and local 
fire district. BOPU would charge a higher rate for outside users (150% percent of City rate) and BOPU 
would not be required to maintain any of the infrastructure related to the special district – so this 
agreement would likely be a net benefit to BOPU.  
 
Annexation of the property is not necessarily straightforward but could present some contiguity 
problems. If annexation did occur, City police, fire, and sanitation services would have to be extended 
well outside their current service boundary to service this subdivision likley resulting in a net fiscal loss. 
Annexation could be accomplished by a ""flagpole annexation" or "annexation off a city island.”   If 
annexation is proposed, City staff will review the proposal to determine any necessary impact fees for 
public safety or other community services. The City would not entertain maintaining any park facilities 
or common areas within the subdivision. 
 
Enabling establishment of lots smaller than five-acres does present planning challenges for future City 
growth. It is inherent that cities grow and at some time Cheyenne will begin to densify to the north.  
Lots smaller than five acres are generally challenging to redevelop.  Lots ranging between one and five 
acres require significant infrastructure to support.  A typical single-family lot street frontage is 60’ and 
the proposed rural lots have frontages four to five times the typical width of a suburban lot.  Single 
family home lots in the City struggle to come close to supporting ongoing maintenance costs for streets, 
utilities, and City support services.  If this property is ever annexed to the City, the subdivision will not be 
able to financially support the maintenance and any necessary upgrades to infrastructure within and 
adjacent to the subdivision.  When the incorporated City reaches this area, development of City-
standard infrastructure extensions to “leapfrog” past this development would place a fiscal burden on a 
developer, or perhaps, the City.         
 
CONCLUSION: 
 
Staff is not satisfied that the application approval is in the best overall interests of the City due. 
 
 
 

UDC-23-00339 
 
 
May 22, 2023 
STAFF RECOMMENDATION: 
 
Staff recommend denial of the proposed outside user agreement by not passing the associated 
resolution. 
 
ATTACHMENTS: 
1. BOPU flow calculation worksheet  
2. County Staff Report  
3. University of Wyoming Center for Business and Economic Analysis study “Evaluating the 
Economic Impact of Providing Municipal Water to Laramie County Housing Developments”

TITLE: 
PUBLIC HEARING regarding a review and recommendation of the Zone 
Change and Preliminary Development Plan for Ironhorse, 3rd Filing, located 
in a portion of the NE1/4 of Section 12, T.14N., R.67W., of the 6th PM, 
Laramie County, WY. 
 
 
 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
 
Palma Land Planning, LLC, on behalf of Ironhorse Capital, has submitted a Preliminary 
Development Plan application to include a zone change to Low Density Residential (LR) for 
Ironhorse, 3rd Filing, located east of Hynds Blvd, west of Yellowstone Rd. and north of W. 
Wrangler Rd. The application has been submitted in order to prepare for clustered residential 
subdivision of approximately 37 lots, 33 residential, 3 open space and 1 vacant lot.  
 
BACKGROUND 
 
There are (4) properties that make up Ironhorse, 3rd Filing all owned by Ironhorse Capital 
Limited Partnership. The contiguous area is vacant with no structures.  The surrounding area 
consists of other residential lots ranging in size from 2.78 to 9.78 acres. 
 
On October 27th 2022 a Preliminary Development Plan, not including the current zone change 
proposal, for Ironhorse 3rd filing was brought forward to the Laramie County Planning 
Commission. The PDP was tabled (5-0) so the applicant could ascertain more detailed 
information for an outside water use agreement with the Cheyenne Board of Public Utilities. In 
order for the configuration of the current PDP to be considered, the applicant would need to have 
an outside water use agreement or a public water and sewer system in place to develop lot sizes 
that are less than 5.25 acres, per the AMEC memo and Development Design Standards. At this 
time a final decision regarding the outside user agreement has not been made, the applicant 
wishes to proceed with a public supply via a community well permitted by the State and 
regulated by the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA).

Pertinent Laramie County Land Use Regulations 
 
Section 2-1-100  governing the requirements for submittal of a Preliminary Development 
 
Plan. 
Section 1-2-103(b) governing required findings of the Planning Commission to 
 
recommend approval of a change in zone district. 
Section 1-2-104(b)(D) governing notice to area property owners for a Preliminary 
 
Development Plan. 
Section 4-2-103 governing the Low Density Residential (LR) Zone District 
 
DISCUSSION 
 
The Laramie County Comprehensive Plan identifies the area as Urban Rural Interface (URI). 
Within this area, higher density residential, intensive commercial, employment centers and 
industrial uses are indicated. Generally, these uses have access to public water and sewer 
services. Adjacent jurisdictions should be involved in decision making in the URI, this is 
important due to the potential water agreement with the City of Cheyenne. While some of these 
areas may include existing agricultural uses, it is anticipated they are more service-oriented and 
may change and develop over time. 
 
PlanCheyenne contemplates Rural Residential uses for this property. Uses include single and 
multi-family residences, generally served by collector or arterial streets.  Supporting and 
complementary uses, including open space recreation, schools, places of worship and other 
public or civic uses are encouraged.  Residential densities vary depending on use and zoning.    
 
According to the Order of the State Engineer for the Laramie County Control Area dated April 1, 
2015, this property currently lies within the Conservation Area, in which the well spacing 
requirements for new permits are indicated at one (1) well for domestic use per each lawfully 
subdivided lot or one (1) per each ten (10) acres of any other legal parcel of land. A letter 
received from the State Engineer’s Office, dated April 11, 2023 which is also attached herein, 
allows for wells to be permitted but strongly suggests that a monitor well be installed down 
gradient to allow for the aquifer impact of this subdivision’s water usage to be studied. 
 
A subdivision of more than 5 lots requires submittal of a Chapter 23 study to the Wyoming 
Department of Environmental Quality (DEQ). A letter received by the Laramie County Board of 
Commissioners on April 20, 2023 indicates provides an adverse recommendation for the 
referenced project meaning that the proposed subdivision does not meet the safety and adequacy 
requirements of DEQ Chapter 23 Rules as submitted, but that conditions listed in attachment 5 
may be placed on the subject septic installations to mitigate impacts. 
   
The subdivision will be accessed primarily from Hynds Blvd., an 80-foot right-of-way from 
Hynds Blvd to Yellowstone Road, being a two lane rural highway. Internal roadways will also 
provide for an 80-foot right-of-way according to the county engineer comments. A traffic study 
has been submitted with the application.

www.laramiecountyplanning.com 
Phone (307) 633-4303     Fax (307) 633-4616 
 
The Environmental and Services Impact Report submitted by the applicant concludes that the 
proposed development will not contribute to any measurable level of negative impacts on the 
area, and that several benefits will be realized for the stakeholders, agencies, rural county 
landowners and the public at-large. 
 
Subsequent to October 27, 2022 Planning Commission meeting, Planning and Development has 
received several inquiries and letters in objection to the proposal, letters to which are contained 
within this package. 
  
RECOMMENDATION and FINDINGS 
 
Based on evidence provided, staff recommends the Planning Commission find that:  
 
a. This application meets the criteria for a preliminary development plan pursuant to 
section 2-1-100 of the Laramie County Land Use Regulations. 
 
and that the Planning Commission may approve the Ironhorse, 3rd Filing, Preliminary 
Development Plan and Zone Change with the following conditions: 
 
1. The applicant shall receive an approved outside user agreement from the City of 
Cheyenne prior to applying for a Zone Change with Subdivision Permit and Plat. 
2. The applicant shall place notes stipulated by the Wyoming Department of Environmental 
Quality on the plat. 
 
 
PROPOSED MOTION 
 
I move to approve the Ironhorse, 3rd Filing, Preliminary Development Plan to include a 
Zone Change to Low Density Residential (LR) and adopt the findings of fact a of the staff 
report with two (2) conditions.

Service District (I&SD). Each well must be pennitted by and constructed to the WQR Chapter 26 Well

•
The information submitted addresses the safety and adequacy of the proposed water system.
PROPOSED SEWAGE SYSTEM GROUNDWATER REVIEW: 
The proposed sewage system consists of individual on-site septic tanks and leach fields constructed to standards 
established in WQR and standards established by the Cheyenne/Laramie County Environmental Health 
Department. The sub-division does overly an unconfined Class I groundwater as defined by WQR Chapter 8 
Rules. 
•
The information submitted addresses the safety and adequacy of the proposed sewage system.
•
The information submitted addresses the safety and adequacy of the proposed sewage system, provided
the statements indicated in the "Notes required to be added to the final plat" <Jre adhered to.
Per WQR Chapter 23 Section 7(d)(viii)(A)(I), WDEQ requires that subdivision applications demonstrate that 
nitrates from on-lot sewage systems will not exceed 10 mg/L at the downgradient property boundary. WDEQ 
recommends including a requirement on the plat that all septic systems be enhanced treatment systems that include 
advanced nitrogen removal technology. Per WQR Chapters 11 and 23, all enhanced treatment systems must be 
permitted and approved by the WDEQ. A Wyoming Registered Professional Engineer must design the enhanced 
treatment systems (WQR Chapter 23, Section 7(d)(viii)(E)). 
Please refer to Appendix A, Groundwater Loading Model analysis located at the end of this letter for additional 
information. 
NOTES REQUIRED TO BE ADDED TO THE FINAL PLAT: 
In order to meet requirements established in Wyoming Water Quality Rules, Chapter 23 Section 
7(d)(viii)(A)(l), individual septic systems are required to be enhanced !reatment systems with 
advanced nitrogen removal technology that is capable of achieving a ·nitrate concentration of less than JO 
mg/L at the downgradient property boundary. 
A Wyoming Registered Professional Engineer must design the enhanced treatment septic system per WQR 
Chapter 23, Section 7(d)(viii)(E). A permit to construct application for enhanced treatment septic systems 
must be submitted to the Southeast District Engineer in Cheyenne. For the proposed Advanced Enviro­
Septic enhanced treatment septic systems the bottom of the leach filed bed sand must be at least 6 feet 
above seasonally high groundwater. 
CONCLUSIONS: 
Based on the materials and information presently submitted for this Chapter 23 subdivision application, the WDEQ 
is issuing an "Adverse" recommendation applicable to the Iron Horse Subdivision. The adverse recommendation is 
due to the detennination that nitrate levels will exceed 10 mg/L at the downgradient property boundary if enhanced 
treatment systems with advanced nitrogen removal are not used. The WDEQ recommends to the County 
Commissioners that the subdivision be approved as presently submitted using enhanced wastewater treatment 
systems. The WDEQ recommends including the Notes Required to Be Added to the Final Plat and proposed 
waslewaler syslems muuifieu lo achieve the standards within WQD Chapter 23 Rules.

April 2023 

2 
 
Center for Business and Economic Analysis  
 
The Center for Business and Economic Analysis (CBEA) at the University of Wyoming 
(UWyo) supports the economic growth and diversification of Wyoming’s economy through 
applied economic and business analytics for communities, industries, and entrepreneurs. The 
center was established in 2019 as a unit within the College of Business. CBEA is a member of 
the Association for University Business and Economic Research (AUBER). 
 
Team 
 
Dr. David Aadland is the Director of the CBEA at UW. He is a professor and former chair of 
the Department of Economics. He teaches Macroeconomics and Econometrics. His research 
interests have been wide and varied over the years. He has written papers in the areas of 
macroeconomics, non-market valuation, applied econometrics, behavioral economics, 
agricultural economics, and statistics. His recent research has focused on interdisciplinary 
natural resource questions and the intersection of ecology, epidemiology, entomology, and 
economics. In particular, he has been working on projects related to pine beetle epidemics, 
ecological impacts of energy development, and the economics of chronic wasting disease. He 
holds a Ph.D. in Economics from the University of Oregon.  
Dr. Anne Alexander served as Interim Provost and Vice President of Academic Affairs at the 
University of Wyoming. She earned her Ph.D. in Economics at UW, and her Master’s and BBA 
in Economics from New Mexico State University. Dr. Alexander’s research and teaching 
focuses on the history of American capitalism, international economics, and health economics. 
She regularly speaks to statewide audiences and state and national media about the status of the 
Wyoming, US, and global economies. She produces an annual economic and statewide 
engagement report on the impacts of the University on the state, and she has authored several 
publications on the political and economic impacts of transboundary pandemics and the 
productivity effects of various diseases. Prior to her position in Academic Affairs, she was the 
Director of International Programs and Associate Dean of Outreach at UW. She has also served 
as a Resources Economist in the U.S. Department of State, Assistant Dean of the UW College of 
Business, and Director of the interdisciplinary Health Economics Policy Center at UW. She is 
an alumni of Leadership Wyoming, Class of 2013. 
Dr. Alex Gebben is an Energy Economist conducting research with the Center for Business and 
Economic Analysis. He holds a B.S. in Petroleum Engineering and graduate degrees in Mineral 
and Energy Economics from the Colorado School of Mines. This background has led him to 
diverse research topics in the areas of ground water management, oil industry incentives, and 
unconventional methods of lithium recovery. He is particularly interested in how the industries 
of oil and gas, wind, geothermal, and nuclear power embody the responsive relationships 
between policy, natural resources, and economic outcomes. Before joining the CBEA he 
conducted research projects in conjunction with the Critical Materials Institute, as well as the 
USDA. Alex is an avid fly fisherman with a love for the outdoors, as well as a self-taught 
programmer. 
 

3 
 
Dr. Rob Godby is an associate professor in the Economics Department at UW, former 
Associate Dean of the Haub School of Environment and Natural Resources, and former interim 
Dean of the College of Business. He also served as the Deputy Director of the University of 
Wyoming’s Center for Energy Regulation and Policy (CERP). Additionally, he serves as a 
Daniel’s Fund Ethics Initiative Faculty Fellow and is an adjunct faculty member with the MBA 
program at Pforzheim University in Germany. Outside his academic duties, Rob was appointed 
to serve on the State of Wyoming’s Consensus Revenue Estimating Group in 2019. His research 
areas include natural resource, energy and environmental economics, industrial organization, 
and macroeconomic policy. He is often interviewed by national and international news media 
on energy and macroeconomic issues. Outside of work, Rob enjoys spending time being walked 
by his dogs. His other passions include sports-car and bicycle racing, both of which he has 
participated in rather unsuccessfully. 
Dr. Morgan Holland graduated with a PhD in economics from Florida State University in 
2022 with research interests in the economics of automation, corporate finance, human capital, 
and the economics of disability. In addition, Morgan worked as an economic consultant for the 
FSU Center for Economic Forecasting and Analysis, pursuing applied research projects in a 
wide variety of fields. Today, Morgan focuses on economic research in the tourism and 
hospitality sectors in Wyoming. He is especially interested in researching and promoting 
policies that will help Wyoming take full advantage of its outdoor recreation resources while 
preserving them for future generations. Morgan also provides support services to stakeholders 
in the tourism and hospitality sector through economic research and analysis. 
 
 
 
Citation: Aadland, D., A. Van Sandt, and S. Nevels. (2023). Evaluating the Economic Impact of 
Providing Municipal Water to Laramie County Housing Developments. Laramie, WY: Center 
for Business and Economic Analysis (CBEA). Prepared for Cheyenne Mayor’s Office.  
 
Correspondence: For additional information, contact aadland@uwyo.edu. 
 

4 
 
Table of Contents 
 
Executive Summary ....................................................................................................................... 5 
1. Introduction  .............................................................................................................................. 6 
2. Costs of Providing Municipal Water  ........................................................................................ 8 
3. Benefits of Providing Muncipal Water  .................................................................................. 11 
4. Spreadsheet Tool ..................................................................................................................... 13 
5. Assumptions, Limitations and Recommendations .................................................................. 15 
 
 

5 
 
Executive Summary 
 
The University of Wyoming Center for Business and Economic Analysis (CBEA) is conducting 
an economic analysis of providing municipal water to housing developments in Laramie 
County.   
 
The analysis shows that … 
 
• for nearly all the parameter values, it does not make sense to provide municipal water to 
Laramie County new housing developments. 
 
• the loss to the city of Cheyenne for a five-acre new housing development is $14,328 or 
$626 per new County resident, using our preferred parameter assumptions. 
 
• the net benefits (i.e., benefits less costs) are negative for all the parameter combinations 
using the interactive spreadsheet tool. 
 
 
 
 
 

6 
 
1. Introduction 
In this project, we calculate the economic costs and benefits of providing municipal water to 
new housing developments in Laramie County, but adjacent to the city of Cheyenne.1  The 
analysis is done from the perspective of the city of Cheyenne and only considers items that lead 
directly to an increase or decrease in the city’s budget.   
 
The costs of providing municipal water are items that either require direct expenditures or lead 
to tax losses for the city of Cheyenne.  The tax losses are associated with a reduction in the 
Cheyenne population when households move from the city to the new housing developments in 
the county.  The reduction in the Cheyenne population relative to the Laramie County 
population reduce the share of tax revenues allocated to the city of Cheyenne.  The items in the 
analysis include: (1) loss of 4% sales tax revenue, (2) loss of 1% sales tax revenue, (3) loss in 
gas tax revenue, (4) loss in severance tax revenue, (5) loss in direct distribution, and (6) 
reduction in the quality of city services. 
 
The benefits of providing municipal water are items that generate more tax revenue for the city 
of Cheyenne.  The items include: (1) one-time 4% sales tax gain from new construction, (2) 
recurring 4% sales tax gain from new residents, (3) one-time 1% sales tax gain from new 
construction, (4) recurring 1% sales tax gain from new residents, and (5) gain in gas tax revenue 
from new residents.   
 
The tax gains and losses are illustrated in Figure 1 below.  The two circles represent the total 
taxes collected, before the new housing development (inner circle) and after the new housing 
development (outer circle).  The slices of the circle represent the portion of tax revenue 
allocated to the city of Cheyenne.  While the new housing development with municipal water 
increases the total tax revenues as new households move into Laramie County, it also decreases 
the share allocated to the city of Cheyenne as people move out of the city to the new housing 
development.  The green shaded area represents the gain in taxes for the city of Cheyenne (i.e., 
more total tax revenues but at a smaller proportion).  The red shaded area represents the loss in 
taxes for the city of Cheyenne (i.e., a smaller proportion of the original tax revenues).   
 
 
 
 
 
 
1 Click here to see a development map of Cheyenne and the surrounding area. 

7 
 
Figure 1.  Tax Gains and Losses to the City of Cheyenne from a New Housing 
Development with Municipal Water 
 
 
 
Sections 2 and 3 below describe the costs and benefits more thoroughly.  Section 4 describes the 
interactive spreadsheet tool, which calculates the net benefits of providing municipal water.  
The spreadsheet allows the user to vary some key parameters and shows the net benefits to the 
city of Cheyenne for two cases:  (1) the status quo without municipal water that requires one 
new house per five acres; and (2) providing municipal water to new housing developments in 
Laramie County.  Section 5 discusses the assumptions, limitations and recommendations 
associated with the analysis. 
 
 
 
 
 
 

8 
 
2. Costs of Providing Municipal Water 
We consider eight different economic costs to the city of Cheyenne if they provide municipal 
water to new housing developments in Laramie County.  We list the economic costs and our 
estimate below.  Each item is then discussed in turn. 
Economic costs of providing municipal water: 
1) Expansion of municipal water infrastructure. 
Estimate:  $0. 
Providing municipal water to housing developments outside the city limits requires 
expenditures on water infrastructure.  We assume these costs are passed along to the 
housing developers and then to the households purchasing the houses.  Therefore, we 
assume the costs to the city are zero. 
 
2) Stress on the municipal water system. 
Estimate:  $0. 
New housing developments outside the city will also require additional water and more 
water treatment.  Based on interviews with the Cheyenne Board of Public Utilities 
(BOPU), there is sufficient capacity right now to add more homes and any costs incurred 
by the city of Cheyenne or BOPU are likely to be compensated for the new homes by 
charging county residents 1.5 times the water rate as city residents.  Therefore, we 
assume the costs to the city are zero. 
 
3) Stress on city services. 
Estimate:  $14,355. 
Just because someone lives just outside of city limits does not mean they cease to use the 
city’s services. These county residents are still likely to recreate in Cheyenne, use police 
and fire services, and the city’s roads. Indeed, many of these county households likely 
conduct their daily economic activity in the city limits of Cheyenne. Unfortunately, 
Cheyenne does not benefit from these residents living just beyond city limits from a tax 
standpoint. Thus, the city of Cheyenne would incur a cost if it sought to maintain the 
same per capita public expenditures as more county residents use its services. To 
estimate Cheyenne’s cost of providing services to county residents we first calculated 
the per household public expenditures by totaling categories in the city’s 2022 adopted 
budget that are reasonably tied to population. For example, within the Mayor 
Department, we may not expect the costs to the mayor division to increase with more 
county residents, but we would expect higher costs to the animal control, compliance, 
municipal court, and youth alternatives divisions. We estimate the status quo 

9 
 
expenditures per household to be $1,466. As more out of county residents are added, this 
figure becomes a per new-county-household cost in order to maintain the same level of 
services. 
 
4) Decrease in 4% state sales tax revenues. 
Estimate:  $6,945. 
If municipal water is provided to housing developments in Laramie County, the portion 
of the 4% sales tax revenue allocated to the city of Cheyenne is likely to fall.  Laramie 
County receives 30% of the 4% sales tax revenue and 70% goes to the state of 
Wyoming.  In 2022, the 30% share amounted to $37M.  The 30% is then divided 
between five cities/county:  Cheyenne, Albin, Burns, Pine Bluffs, and Unincorporated 
Laramie County, as a proportion of their respective populations.  Therefore, as 
households move from the city to the new development in the county, the city of 
Cheyenne receives a smaller portion of the 4% sales tax (i.e., the red shaded area in 
Figure 1 above).     
 
5) Decrease in 1% city sales tax revenues. 
Estimate:  $5,631. 
Similar to the tax loss in item #4 above, the city of Cheyenne loses a portion of the 1% 
sales tax revenue.  In 2022, the 1% sales tax generated $30M for Laramie County 
without any portion going to the state.  The $30M is then divided in the same fashion 
described in item #4 above.  
 
6) Decrease in gas tax revenues. 
Estimate:  $10. 
The city of Cheyenne also loses gas tax revenues if households residing in Cheyenne 
move to the new housing development in Laramie County.  However, the allocation rule 
for the gas tax is different than sales taxes.  15% of the gas tax revenue is allocated to 
municipalities with Cheyenne’s portion weighted by 75% from gas tax distributors in 
Cheyenne and 25% from Cheyenne’s portion of the total incorporated population of 
Wyoming.  In 2022, the total allocation was $1.59M.  The loss in taxes associated with 
the new housing development is due to having a smaller portion of Wyoming’s 
incorporated population as households move from Cheyenne to unincorporated Laramie 
County (i.e., see shaded red area in Figure 1). 
 
 
 
 
 

10 
 
7) Decrease in severance tax revenues. 
Estimate:  $53. 
The city of Cheyenne received $2.2M in severance taxes from the state of Wyoming in 
20222.  The total allocation to all incorporated cities was $15.656M in 2022.  If a new 
five-acre housing development with municipal water is created in Laramie County and 
half of the houses purchased are from Cheyenne residents, then the loss in severance 
taxes to the city of Cheyenne would be $53.  This is illustrated by the shaded red area in 
Figure 1. 
 
 
8) Decrease in direct distribution from the state of Wyoming. 
Estimate:  $833. 
The direct distribution of tax revenue from the state of Wyoming to the city of Cheyenne 
in 2022 was approximately $4.8M.  The allocation mechanism uses the Madden-Bebout 
rule.  The Madden-Bebout rule specifies that 75% of the distribution is from sales tax 
per capita and 25% of the distribution is from assessed valuation per capita plus a fixed 
amount of $35,000.  If a new five-acre housing development is created and half of the 
houses are purchased by Cheyenne residents, then the direct distribution to Cheyenne 
would decrease by $833. 
 
Total Economic Cost to the City of Cheyenne:  $27,808 
 

11 
 
3. Benefits of Providing Municipal Water 
 
We consider five different economic benefits to the city of Cheyenne if they provide municipal 
water to new housing developments in Laramie County.  We list the economic benefits below 
and discuss each in turn. 
Economic benefits of providing municipal water: 
1) One-time 4% sales tax increase from new house construction. 
 
Estimate: $4,535. 
 
When new homes are built, they create local economic opportunities for both labor and 
suppliers of input materials. While the city and county do not encounter direct tax 
revenue from the labor, they do collect on the sales tax of the input material suppliers. 
We estimate the value of these input materials by modeling a new home construction in 
the economic contribution software, IMPLAN. First, we assume that an average new 
home in Cheyenne costs $267,000 to build. This estimate was calculated by cross-
referencing the average construction cost per square foot in Cheyenne and multiplying it 
by the average home size in Cheyenne. Given this initial construction cost, IMPLAN 
estimates the total value added to Laramie County’s economy by calculating all 
purchases not only from the home’s construction, but also the purchases of new labor 
used to build the home. In total, IMPLAN estimates a value added contribution of 
$464,029.21 from new home construction in Laramie County. Multiplying this figure by 
the 4% sales tax and Cheyenne’s new (lower) tax distribution results in Cheyenne’s 
benefit from the construction of a new home in the county. Since a new home is only 
built once, this economic contribution only takes place in the year the home is built. 
 
2) Recurring 4% sales tax increase from new county residents. 
 
Estimate:  $2,731. 
 
While more residents living in Laramie County relative to the city of Cheyenne would 
lead to a sales tax distribution that favors the county more, total sales tax revenue could 
increase due to new residents moving to the county. In Figure 1, this concept is 
represented by the green area. Unlike new home construction, this tax revenue would be 
a recurring source of revenue for the city and county since the overall population of the 
county and related sales activity would likely increase and stay at these new higher 
levels, assuming no additional shocks. This means that there is potential for the city of 
Cheyenne to benefit from new home construction in the county if the negative 
distributional effect (red area in Figure 1) is counteracted by more out-of-county people 
moving into the new homes (green area in Figure 1). 
 
 

12 
 
3) One-time 1% sales tax increase from new house construction. 
 
Estimate:  $3,779. 
 
The one-time 1% increase in sales tax revenue from new house construction is similar to 
the benefits in item #1 above.  However, all the revenue from the penny tax goes to 
Laramie County. 
 
 
4) Recurring 1% sales tax increase from new county residents. 
 
Estimate:  $2,214. 
 
The recurring 1% increase in sales tax revenue from new county residents is similar to 
the benefits in item #2 above.  However, all the revenue from the penny tax goes to 
Laramie County. 
 
5) Gas tax increase due to new residents. 
 
Estimate:  $221. 
 
Since IMPLAN takes into account input suppliers’ purchases of gas, the only additional 
increases in gas tax revenue come from new residents’ purchases of gas. This means that 
this tax benefit is incurred every year the new residents live in the county. We calculate 
this by multiplying the per capita gas tax revenue by the number of new residents in the 
county.  
 
 
Total Economic Benefit to the City of Cheyenne:  $13,479  
 
Table 1. Economic Costs and Benefits of Providing Municipal Water 
Total ($) 
Total per Person ($) 
Costs 
$27,808 
$1,214 
Benefits 
$13,479 
$589 
Net Benefits 
($14,328) 
($626) 
 
 
 

13 
 
4. Spreadsheet Tool 
We also provide an interactive spreadsheet tool which allows the user to see the economic costs 
and benefits.  The tool allows the user to vary key parameters to see how the net benefits (i.e., 
economic benefits less costs) change as the parameter values are changed.   
Figure 2.  Interactive Spreadsheet Tool 
We also provide an interactive spreadsheet tool which allows the user to see the economic costs 
and benefits.  The tool allows the user to vary key parameters to see how the net benefits (i.e., 
economic benefits less costs) change as the parameter values are changed.   
Figure 2.  Interactive Spreadsheet Tool 
 
Above is a picture of the layout of the interactive spreadsheet. The purpose of the tool is to 
allow for easy visualization of the results of the analysis and for varying the parameters for 
different conditions.  
The spreadsheet is divided into four primary regions. In the top left are the costs we take into 
account and their values based on the parameter levels set in the tool. In the top right are the 
benefits derived from the chosen parameter levels. In the bottom right are the parameters used 
in calculating the aforementioned costs and benefits. Two of the parameters we hold fixed; the 

14 
 
number of people per household and the number of houses on 5 acres. The value of the people 
per household parameter comes from the US Census’ data on the city of Cheyenne. The number 
of houses on 5 acres, if municipal water is provided, we assume to be 10. This accounts for 
roads and other infrastructure in the development that is not part of individual lots. Two 
parameters are directly variable; the migration percentage from the city and the number of acres 
being considered for development. The migration percentage affects the values of the costs and 
benefits emanating from taxation by changing the population of Cheyenne relative to the 
county. A value of 100% indicates that all residents of the new homes have emigrated from 
Cheyenne, while a value of 0% indicates that all the residents are from outside the area. At the 
bottom of the parameters table are two buttons that set the plan being considered in the tool: 
either no provision or municipal water or provision of municipal water. Clicking between these 
varies the number of houses on 5 acres and resets all the parameter values to base assumptions. 
Finally, in the bottom left is an interactive graph showing the sizes of the costs and benefits 
relative to each other and the net benefit per resident of the new homes. 
 
 
 

15 
 
Assumptions and Recommendations 
 
We made several different assumptions in order to calculate the economic costs and benefits.  
The assumptions include: 
 
• The costs and benefits of the new housing development are solely from the perspective 
of the city of Cheyenne. 
 
• We assume the houses in the new development are purchased by an equal share of 
Cheyenne residents and households from outside Laramie County, WY. 
 
• The benefits and costs scale up and down proportional to the size of the housing 
development. 
 
• The benefits and costs are independent of the location of the housing development. 
 
• The infrastructure, maintenance and water supply costs are covered by charging 1.5 
times the water rate of Cheyenne residents. 
 
• The analysis does not consider the option value of leaving the land undeveloped.  This 
may be an important consideration for a city that has limited areas where it can expand 
to allow for future population growth. 
 
Based on the assumptions above and our sensitivity analysis, our recommendation is for the city 
of Cheyenne to not provide municipal water to new housing developments in Laramie County.  
Although we recognize it might be politically complicated, an alternative solution is to annex 
the land occupied by the new housing development into the city of Cheyenne.  In this way, the 
costs to the city of Cheyenne could be covered by the additional tax revenues, but Laramie 
County would receive the benefits of a larger population.

# SOURCE_FILE: CHEY-2023-05-02_TotalExtraction_88008346.pdf
Planning and Development Office
82001
(Phone) 307-637-6282  
(Fax) 307-637-6366
MEMO
To:
Honorable Mayor Patrick Collins and Members of the City Council
From:
Charles W. Bloom, AICP, Planning and Development Director
Seth C. Lloyd, Senior Planner
Subject:
Outside User Agreement 3730 Rawlins Street, Laramie County, Wyoming (Located 
northwest of the intersection of Rawlins Street and McCann Avenue)
Date:
May 22, 2023
Background: 
The item for consideration is an Outside User Agreement.  This is approved by resolution.  Typically, a 
resolution is introduced and referred to committee prior to final consideration.  If this resolution 
followed the standard process, final consideration would be June 12, 2023.  
The applicant has indicated they an emergency exists due to the septic system being damaged by others 
which has resulted in an immediate health hazard. Specifically, the septic system
was 
located on an adjacent parcel under different ownership and the new owner has damaged the leach 
field to a point where the system does not function. Staff requests that this item be considered upon 
introduction and not referred to committee due to the extenuating circumstances.  
This property is 100% surrounded by City limits, and it is 
to initiate the annexation of all County pockets. If an annexation petition were filed by the landowner by 
the next submittal deadline, May 24th, the annexation would not be complete until mid-August.  Filing
would only be possible if the applicant had managed to create the necessary annexation maps required 
for filing and paid all application fees ($2,275). City-initiated annexations take time and staff resources 
to complete, and while the parcel will be annexed to the City in the next year or so, the health issue 
created by the removal of a portion of the septic system creates more urgency for this parcel. Even if 
City Staff suddenly focused on this parcel to annex, the timelines involved for either annexation scenario
would mean it would be months before annexation was complete.
Approval of this resolution would allow for the property owner to immediately connect to City sewer.  
BOPU has indicated that the standard outside user agreement fee (150%) would still apply; however; 
the additional 1/3rd of the cost would be refunded to the landowner if the property is annexed by the 
City or the applicant within two years of resolution approval.  
A reimbursement agreement for public infrastructure improvements for street and sewer was filed on 
this property on April 19, 2012.  Connection to City sewer or landowner-initiated annexation is 
considered a development action under the prior reimbursement notice of infrastructure 
reimbursement. The 2012 infrastructure costs assigned to this lot were $8,939.39 for streets and 
Planning and Development Department
(Phone) 307-637-6282 (Fax) 307-637-6366

$4,704.84 for sewers. Payment will be subject to the computation of this value to be reimbursed by 
using the construction cost index for twenty (20) cities, as issued by the current Engineering News 
Record, at the date of construction compared to the construction cost index at the date of 
reimbursement. This is $12,819.54 for streets and $6,746.98 in today s dollars. 
 
There are questions surrounding this property and the now separated northern portion, specifically, 
how they were subdivided.  
he property at 3730 Rawlins Street is 
outside of City limits and was originally developed with a well and septic system. In 2008, a portion of 
the septic system servicing this address was annexed to the City (Ord #3821, bk 2078 pg 380), the 
annexation resulted in the creation of two tax parcels. The creation of two tax parcels created an 
assumption the two tax parcels could be sold off separately, and a previous landowner did indeed sell 
off the properties separately.    
 
The proposed Governing Body review schedule is as follows: 
 
Introduction and final consideration   May 22, 2023 
 
Attachments: 
1. Resolution 
 

RESOLUTION NO. __________ 
ENTITLED: "A RESOLUTION APPROVING SANITARY SEWER SERVICE, BY 
OUTSIDE USER AGREEMENT, FOR MYERS, AUSTIN ET AL, FOR THE 
PROPERTY AT
3730 RAWLINS STREET, LARAMIE COUNTY, 
WYOMING (LOCATED NORTHWEST OF THE INTERSECTION OF 
RAWLINS STREET AND MCCANN AVENUE)." 
 
 
WHEREAS, Myers, Austin Et Al ("the Owner") is the owner of the property in Laramie 
County, Wyoming, more specifically described as: the southerly 127' of Lot 1, Block 1, Rogers 
Subdivision, Laramie County, Wyoming (per annexation Ord #3821, bk 2078 pg 380), addressed 
as 3730 Rawlins Street; 
WHEREAS, the Owner’s property is outside of City limits and was originally developed 
with a well and septic system; 
WHEREAS, a portion of the Owner’s septic system was on a parcel to the north of their 
current parcel which was annexed to the City in 2008 (Ord #3821, bk 2078 pg 380), said 
annexation resulting in the creation of two parcels for tax purposes; 
 
 
WHEREAS, the creation of two tax parcels created an assumption the two tax parcels 
could be sold off separately, which assumption was acted upon by a previous landowner which 
did indeed sell off the properties separately; 
 
 
WHEREAS, the owner of the northern parcel has removed the portion of the Owner’s 
septic system on their property, creating a health hazard on the Owner’s property; 
 
 
WHEREAS, it is the City of Cheyenne City Council’s stated goal to initiate the annexation 
of all County pockets (land 100% surrounded by City limits, but not within the City’s jurisdiction).  
However, such annexations take time and resources to complete; 
 
 
WHEREAS, the subject property is a County pocket (land 100% surrounded by City 
limits, but not within the City), therefore it is only a matter of time before the subject property will 
be annexed to the City; 
 
 
WHEREAS, pursuant to Resolution No. 3577, BOPU was instructed to present to the 
Governing Body for its review any application for the provision of water and/or sanitary sewer 
services to any individual or entity outside the corporate limits of the City of Cheyenne; 
 
 
WHEREAS, the Owner will approve and execute applicable Outside User Agreement(s) 
upon approval by the Governing Body pursuant to Resolution No. 3577 and application of the 
Owner if approved by the BOPU Board; and, 
 

WHEREAS, the Governing Body has reviewed the potential for water and/or sanitary 
sewer services on the Owner’s property and has determined that approval is in the best interest of 
the City of Cheyenne. 
 
 
NOW, THEREFORE BE IT RESOLVED BY THE GOVERNING BODY OF THE 
CITY OF CHEYENNE, WYOMING, THAT, water and/or sanitary sewer services outside of 
the corporate limits of the City of Cheyenne for the property addressed as 3730 Rawlins Street, 
Laramie County, Wyoming, is hereby approved. 
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that BOPU and the Owner are authorized to approve 
and execute Outside User Agreement(s). 
 
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that BOPU is directed to refund 1/3 of the system 
development fees and taps fees if the property is annexed within two (2) years of the adoption of 
this Resolution. 
 
PRESENTED, READ AND ADOPTED this _______ day of _____________________, 
2023. 
 
 
__________________________________________  
Patrick Collins, Mayor 
 
 
(SEAL)  
 
 
ATTEST: 
 
____________________________________  
Kristina F. Jones, City Clerk
